ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e95	Honourable co-representatives! Former agent Marie Brenden is dead, 74 years old. It was engagement in the local environment that led Marie Brenden into politics. Everything that could contribute to making Saksumdal a better rural community became the arena of Marie's multiplicity. Here she rebuilt the work force, started kindergarten, became a librarian, a proponent for new kindergarten, director of the sports team and Saksumdal Building. Marie Brenden was representative of Oppland and the Workers' Party from 1985 to 1997. During the three periods she was elected, she was a member of the Social Committee, the Justice Committee and the Defence Committee. She also met in the enlarged Foreign Affairs Committee, was a delegate to the UN General Assembly and a delegate to the Council of Europe Parliamentary Assembly. Engaged as she was, Brenden held many public positions and positions of trust in the party and organisations. A position she particularly appreciated was as a member of the Board of Directors of the Lillehammer Olympic Organisation Committee (LOOC) in 1988-1993. Her involvement in sports also benefited her colleagues and employees at Stortinget. She held the executive board of the Stortinges Sports Act four of her twelve years as a magnificent representative. Marie Brenden was a genuine political man. She was a politician many listened to before they drew their conclusions in difficult matters. She was, like, always at the center of the big group's opinions. Marie Brenden had a lukewarmness and gladly contributed with good lines. She was a self-righteous person who would all well, a person who had the ability to see you, and who could listen. Not getting elected and employees of the Workers' Group chose to listen to Marie's wise words and judgements. After contributing to national and county policy, she again became involved in local policy. Until recently, she showed a social exposure that was respected. She was particularly concerned that even those who were a little worse off than most had to be taken care of. We thank Marie Burnden for her work and brighten the peace of her memory. The representatives heard the President's commemorative speech.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e245	The data protection has not been forgotten. It is our main tool for day-to-day data protection in the administration. It helps us keep order in the private sector and in the Norwegian public in general. Supervision deals with issues that are very important to citizens. Data protection helps us by making us aware of areas of particular risk and can also provide advice on how to reduce the harm potential of personal data. The Committee has also highlighted on previous occasions the importance of supervisory advisory work in relation to the various social sectors and organisations there, in order to develop together good industry standards. The development of professional cutbacks that go beyond the minimum legal requirements for the processing of personal data may supplement formal laws and regulations, while reminding stakeholders that they should not necessarily exploit the technical possibilities at the extreme limit of the law. The Committee would also point out that the practical experience gained by the Data Protection Supervisor through its activities represents a knowledge centre for data protection issues which the legislator can use in the development of new rules, when and if technological developments so require. Therefore, the Ombudsman’s role as a participant in the societal debate is welcome. We also see that the issues that the supervision identifies for us through the Annual Report are important messages to the Stortinget as legislator. The Committee took note in its annual communication that there is a movement in international data protection. Both in the EU, the OECD and the Council of Europe there is a review of international standards on privacy. In fact, legal developments in the EU during this reporting period — but also in this year — have given rise to some optimism. For my part, I want to record that the implementation of the Data Storage Directive has stopped, which was so very important to many, is now suspended. At the same time, the new initiative that approaches the issues from the other side – how can we extend citizens' privacy? In January this year, the European Commission proposed a completely new regulatory framework for the modernisation of EU data protection rules. It is a general regulation on the protection of personal data, and it should therefore replace the Data Protection Directive, which has been implemented in Norway in the past by the Data Protection Act of 2000. There are in particular four principles that are essential here: first, that you strengthen the right to be forgotten. You have to make it easier to move data from one provider to another, where the first one, like this. has lost a customer relationship, is required to delete information. Thirdly, there is a requirement for documentation, which moves a little further away from the concessional process and above to demonstrate good practices for the processing of sensitive personal data. Then it's also – quite reasonable – made simpler rules for smaller businesses. This is a development of justice that I think is very exciting. We have not tabled a major and long general debate on privacy issues today, and the Committee's attitude reflects the broad consensus on the importance of privacy. Nevertheless, we have to admit that when we come under the general assessments that everyone can agree on, there appeared to be a somewhat different balance between government and opposition, which in the Committee is represented with the Right Party, the Christian People's Party and the Advance Party. There are two points in the setting that we have separated a little layer. One is that, in particular, the members of the Committee of the Advancement Party, the Right Party and the Christian People's Party have highlighted the problems of institutions and schools not being able to hold class lists internally. The second is that the same members have highlighted how good it is that tax information now does not move freely around in cyberspace. The government has probably been pressured into this by an opinionary pressure, but there's of course government parties have a different opinion.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e260	The annual processing of annual reports by the Data Protection Supervisor and the Subject provides the Stortinget with an opportunity to discuss privacy. The rapporteur has given a good account of the Committee's work on the matter. Technological developments are moving rapidly. It's not that many years since we talked about EDB, electronic data processing. Today, the term is out of date because there is hardly any electronic data processing. On 25 January, the European Commission presented proposals for the modernisation of EU standards of protection. Today’s data protection legislation is largely from 1995, when less than one per cent of Europeans used the Internet. In October, together with the Progress Party of Hans Frode Kielland Asmyhr, I attended a meeting between the European Parliament and national parliaments in Europe on the reform. Norwegian media give the impression that the discussion in the EU is moving towards a reduced level of privacy. That's the opposite that happens. The proposed reform strengthens individual rights. These include: the right to be forgotten and consent to the retention of personal data. Because technology is international, we need rules to ensure cross-border privacy. The Data Protection Authority has participated in the preparatory work on the new EU data protection legislation. It is good, and it is essential that the Data Protection Authority also has the opportunity and priority to participate in the discussions in the EU in the future. It is important that supervision is up-to-date in the field of technological development and international thinking in the field of privacy. In the reporting year 2011, the Stortinge decided to implement the Data Storage Directive. I will not go into the matter again, but only note that parts of the debate inside and outside the Storting had a form we are hardly served with. Of course, our neighbouring countries, which have introduced the Directive, are not ‘totalitarian monitoring communities’. That being said, the debate in recent years on privacy has been valuable. It has raised awareness of the importance of privacy and how this is challenged by changing technology. I would also like to comment briefly on one of the opposition's comments — the tax information: for the Workers' Party, transparency about tax information is important. For all adherents of democracy and the liberal economy, transparency about economic conditions should be a matter of course. There are also a number of examples that searchlights on equation numbers have given regulatory changes, for example: in the fight against zero-taxors, taxes on real wealth, higher taxes on capital incomes that are actually employee incomes, and focus on high incomes across industries, such as lawyers and brokers. However, new technologies have led to the dissemination and use of these tax information, which in some cases have crossed the threshold of what we should accept for privacy reasons and which have opened up abuses in criminal activities. The Facebook application that could sort your friends by income is an example of this. The Workers' Party believes that the changes The Stortinget made in paragraphs 8 to 8 of the Equation Act in 2011, are correct and necessary and that today's legislation ensures a reasonable balance between the different considerations. The personal data contained in the tax lists are as public as before, but privacy considerations are much better addressed. In the 2013 state budget, the government proposes 37.9 million. kr for the operation of the Data Protection Authority. This means that since 2005, government parties have increased the Data Protection Framework by 72 pst. In addition, over the same period, supervision has been added to a total of 13,45 million. kr in project funds. It is an expression of the priority we give to privacy and the important job done by the Data Protection Authority.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e277	Both the representative of Tetzschner and the representative of Haugli have done well for the committee to deal with this matter, and as Mr Tetzschner says, there is a great deal of agreement in the committee on this matter. I only want to link some comments to what is involved in the electronic exchange of messages between healthcare professionals. If you are travelling today and you move from your home to another part of the country and suddenly get so sick that you have to enter a hospital quickly, and you are unable to do things for yourself, then the hospital you are admitted to has great difficulty in getting information about your health status. The reason is that there is currently no communication between hospitals about the health situation of people. Only 12 out of 429 municipalities have taken part in an electronic message exchange with healthcare professionals. This means that there is a great risk that a person may get out of hospital properly when the hospital to which he or she is admitted does not get any idea if he or she has had an operation, is allergic or may be using medication that could be dangerous for any other intervention on the patient. The representatives from Rogaland, Hordaland and Sogn and Fjordane have annual meetings with Health West. Last week we were informed by representatives from Health Vest that work is now being done to develop guidelines so that one could have the ability to transfer personal information from local authorities and local authorities to other hospitals, so that one could be able to capture the health situation in another hospital in a very fast way – if one is admitted to another hospital. But then it's also very important that privacy is in place, so that personal information cannot get out of control, and that people other than those who need it can get personal information that should not come out to anyone but those who will benefit them. Therefore, the Progress Party is pleased that the Data Protection Authority is doing a brilliant job, and we feel reasonably confident that, in this situation too, the Data Protection Authority will be able to follow up the reorganisation of information between medical agencies and healthcare professionals in a defensive, safe and secure way.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e299	Technological development offers enormous opportunities, but also significant challenges, when it comes to privacy. More and more are being digitized, and large parts of our lives are going on and on portals and in databases around the world. Technology characterizes a great many of our daily tasks. Increasingly more information is stored, and we leave electronic tracks almost no matter what we do and where we go. Some of us even have cell phones that can explain where we are at any time. In addition, information about what we bought, and when we bought it, and if we use bank cards or credit cards, that's the reality. It's recorded when and where we've been through the bombings. Even travel routes to and from work are recorded. Most of this information is available on the Internet. The Internet is a great tool for finding and sharing information, but also an arena where more personal information is involuntaryly open. This information was previously available, including: tax information on all taxable persons in the country – an information that contributed to both harassment and crime, but was also important to ensure parts of the transparency and legitimacy of the tax system. With such a huge, and growing, continuous flow of personal data, there is a great, and growing, need for ever stronger privacy. It is important that we address the challenges such as: an increasingly electronic everyday life brings with it. People need to be informed about their rights. Here is the role of the Data Protection Authority as a knowledge intermediary. Good web pages and other user-friendly services are a "must" to enable us all to know what we can demand of privacy, and how we can organise ourselves so that we can avoid our personal data being stolen or abused. Privacy is under pressure and new technologies allow for increased control, registration and monitoring. New technology always offers new opportunities, but it is policy's role to limit technology when there are conflicts of interest here, against privacy. I am sceptical and critical of the ever more extensive monitoring, registration and control. The more information is stored, the more opportunities it offers for misuse and information leakage. The emergence of the information society requires that we have a clear and principled view of privacy and individual inviolability. What is human worth? What are the rights of individual citizens? How do we relate to an information flow that increasingly challenges the individual's right to privacy? In recent years, privacy has been pushed several times. The Data Storage Directive is one example that can be drawn up, and the changes in the laws supported by large parts of the Right and the entire Workers' Party have been undermined by some of our fundamental data protection principles. We are still faced with similar problems. New issues will emerge, and in many cases there will be good reasons to introduce stricter controls. That's the sum of things that make us gradually and almost inconspicuously slip in the wrong direction. The sum of good intentions can make a go in a direction no one really wants, and to a society where human dignity is violated. Consideration must always be weighed, but if we do not have a conscious relationship with the basic principles here, we will almost continue to drift away from basic privacy. I have noticed that the government has previously invited Stortinget to a complete and collective discussion of privacy issues, and that at the same time it has been notified that there will be a separate notice to the Stortinget about privacy. Of course, it is disappointing that such a message has not come. Such a message is particularly important now that several major cases have weakened privacy in recent years. I know that the Council of State has had a lot to do lately, but I hope that she can nevertheless make sure that such a message will preferably be available by the end of this period.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e325	Technological developments are fast – very fast. Nordics are eager to use new technologies and new opportunities. We use our smartphones to surf the web, to keep ourselves up to date on social media, to pay travel in public transport or to find the way through map solutions – and much more. In almost everything we do, we leave behind a trail that tells us in detail what we've done, and where we've been during the day. ‘Big data’ is a term used for large amounts of electronic tracks that we leave behind in a day. These are tracks that can be collated and analysed. This challenges the privacy of the individual. The controllers must inform about the personal data for which they process and what this information is to be used for. Besides, we all have to be aware of what information we leave behind, and how this information can be used by others. At the same time, technology opens up great opportunities. We can provide simpler services for citizens, services that can be used anywhere, at any time. It is the government's aim to provide the best possible digital services in the administration. We want to use the opportunities offered by digitisation to protect the privacy of users. Privacy by Design – or built-in privacy – is called it. Digital transparency solutions, electronic reporting of the data subject or the ability to update information itself may be relevant measures. We can provide new digital solutions to personal alternatives such as integrated first choice, for example: by requiring no more information than is strictly necessary. If the individual wishes to provide additional information, he or she must take the initiative. In the 2011 Annual Communication, the Data Protection Authority points to data protection challenges in public information. The publication of tax lists is an example of this. This year, access to the tax registers has been slightly tighter than in previous years. The tax lists are still public. They should be. At the same time, the privacy of taxpayers is better protected because the lists may not be used to the same extent as before for entertainment, sheikhs or crime planning. The press has not been allowed to publish the entire electronic tax list on their websites. Transparency requires MinID login. The new rules show that technology opens up opportunities for better privacy solutions. Data minimisation is good privacy. This means that no more personal data is collected than the purpose requires. Perhaps the aim can also be achieved with anonymous solutions. Electronic ticketing in public transport is a good example. The industry and the authorities have jointly developed a professional standard for anonymous electronic ticketing. Passengers may choose whether to travel anonymously or to identify themselves. In recent years, the rapporteur has been reviewing international legislation. This applies both in the OECD, in the Council of Europe and, not least, in the EU. We are using more and more online services. They can be offered from anywhere in the world, even if users are in Norway. The services require the flow of personal data. As a result, privacy challenges are also becoming more international. A good international framework can be of great help in addressing the challenges. It is also an advantage both for providers and for users if the legislation in the different countries is as uniform as possible. Therefore, it is good that the Data Protection Authority prioritises international work. The government is also involved in international data protection work in order to safeguard Norwegian interests. The examination of data protection consequences shall be a natural part of the processing of all personal data. Through an active approach to privacy issues, we shall be in the forefront and maintain the privacy of our citizens in a good way. I look forward to a good debate on how to safeguard the privacy of our citizens when we debate next year the communication on privacy, as the government will present later this year.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e427	The state of Norway is established in the territory of two peoples, Samer and Nordmann. Both peoples have the same right and the same right to be able to develop their culture and language. Samemen is an indigenous people who have a call for special cultural protection under international law. The merger is therefore also established by the joint law in order to comply with the Constitution § 110a. This gives the whole thing a unique position as a popularly elected body with great freedom. The 2011 Joint Annual Report sets out the work of the Sameting, the tasks for which the Sameting shall manage and the economic and administrative challenges. The communication covers a wide range of areas in which Sametinget works — ranging from work on discrimination and equality, co-operation across national borders, regional policy issues, land and environment policy issues1 through culture, cultural protection and language, to kindergartens, schools and the preservation of traditional knowledge. It is not possible in a brief article to address everything that concerns the Sami communities. So I have to draw something out. It is not that everything that is not mentioned is not considered important. It is the whole Committee, with the exception of the Progress Party, which is united on the majority comments. I expect the Progress Party itself to take the floor to explain its divergent views on the matter. By way of introduction, I need to commend the whole thing for the job that has been done in recent years to clean up aspects of administrative practices. It concerns both financial management, transparency and transparency. Among other things, the Sametinget has now also put in place a system of postal records online. This contributes to the direct transparency of, and greater transparency of, the activities of the Sameting. It's a pleasure. The consultation mechanism has become a good tool for dialogue between the State and the Sameting. There is no consensus in all matters, but in the majority of cases there is agreement or partial agreement. The institute has now evolved over some time, and seems to work well after the intention. It makes an important contribution to the security of Sami interests. In its annual report, the merger addresses matters relating to consultations with the Ministry of Agriculture. The proposition refers to the fact that the House Bank provides basic loans for the repair, construction and purchase of housing, start-up loans to young people and disadvantaged people and loans to kindergartens. Basic loans shall contribute to the promotion of living qualities such as universal design and energy saving in new and existing dwellings. By 31 August it was given approx. 7 B.C.E. kr in the amount of the basic loan. The high demand for mortgages includes, among other things: coherence with high and increasing activity in the construction sector in 2012. There's a reason to enjoy. Figures for the third quarter of 2012 show that a start-up permit was granted for the construction of a total of 8,107 dwellings. It's almost 38 pst. more dwellings than in the third quarter of last year. The most important thing we do in housing policy is to facilitate the construction of more dwellings. The bulk of the framework increase will therefore be used to meet the high demand for mortgages. Starting loans are given to help those who are struggling to establish themselves in owner-occupied dwellings. By 31 August it was given approx. 5.7 B.C.E. kr in initial loans. The loans to kindergartens were granted for the construction of new, privately owned kindergartens, and by 31 August it was given approx. 0.8 B.C.E. kr in kindergarten loans. When they make demands on the banks, they do their job. The opposition points out in its comments, inter alia: until ‘Rights have dismissed this sharpening’. Does this mean that the parties believe it should be entirely up to the banks to grant loans without a claim for equity? I have so far understood the opposition so that they are not opposed to an equity claim, but they believe the limit should go at 10 and not 15 pst. Why should the limit go right there? With the logic they apply, banks should not be free to deviate from claims for 10 or 15 pst. ? Keeping order in the economy is a primary objective of the government and of the Workers' Party. In housing policy, there are no easy solutions. If more people get a place to live, they have to build more. When dealing with the next case, we are given the opportunity to discuss housing policy more generally. The government's goal is for everyone to live well and safely. The house bank is the state's main tool in housing policy. Extending the lending framework – as we do today – is one of several contributions to increased construction activity and improved quality of living in Norway. I am pleased that the committee has unanimously joined the government's proposal.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e781	Four of the representatives of the Right have proposed measures for increased housing. The proposers are committed to facilitating people to live in a place, to building enough homes, and to afford most people to live in the dwellings that are being built. That's what I think we all agree on. Before I go into the individual proposals, I would like to say three things about housing in Norway. First, we're in a period of historically high population growth. In March we stayed five million in this country. This is particularly marked in and around the largest cities. It is fundamentally positive that people have children, that the Norwegian economy is going so well, that household income is rising, and that we are attracting labour from abroad, but it also leads to an increased need for new homes. The forecasting centre calculates that the annual housing demand in Norway is approximately 38 000 new dwellings per year. Secondly, construction activity is on its way up. This year we expect it to be completed approx. 30000 new dwellings. Since the mid-1980s, we have only had the same number of new dwellings for two single years, in 1988 and 2007. Nevertheless, the supply does not match the demand. Evolution goes in the right direction, but it has to be built more. Thirdly, in the period 2001–05, it was built on average for 22 000 dwellings per year. This then increased to 31 000 in 2008. In 2009, it fell again to 21,763. The slowdown came as a consequence of the financial crisis. And yet, even in the 2009 crisis, more dwellings were completed than in 2003. So it's not like the housing structure has slowed down under this government, on the contrary. There are, on average, much more dwellings built in the year than in the previous ageing period, despite the financial crisis. Lack of political will in some municipalities, productivity and capacity challenges in the construction industry, lack of finished land for housing purposes and insufficient overall land and transport planning are among the reasons why it is not built even more. The Labour Party takes the challenges in the housing market seriously. We are among others concerned about people's ability to settle in owner-occupied dwellings. However, there is no quick fix. We are concerned that action needs to be seen in the context. Not all of the original proposals have been maintained. I only want to comment on those who have to vote for it later today. The first three proposals concern the State's right of objection. As long as the municipalities are responsible for finalising regulatory plans, there is a need for an institution of opposition to ensure national and regional considerations. If two neighbouring municipalities were to decide without object where the roads and the railways were going, we would most likely have many roads and railways that were not very well connected. That being said, we take seriously the dissatisfaction with the right of objection. We believe that the solution lies in better coordination at state level. Objection access is an important safety valve, but municipalities must meet a more coordinated country. As regards proposal No: 4, agrees on the need for measures that may shorten the planning period for large-scale transport projects. The Minister for Transport has launched a package of measures: deadlines, coordination at an early stage and parallel planning processes. This is a good initiative. Finally, I would like to comment on the Right's proposal to lift the ban on low-income housing laws. The rules that it can be a maximum of 75 pst. public debt following the formation of a new law on housing and the prohibition of so-called buying-in of speculation were adopted on 14 June last year. It ensures a secure framework for the purchase of housing and prevents people in the worst cases from being blamed and their carers from being able to serve. There was broad consensus from key actors on the need for legislative change, including NBBL, and the decision was taken by a broad majority, all parties except the Right and the Left. Although there is disagreement about much of what is proposed, I would like to thank the proposers for bringing the debate on housing policy into the main hall. The recommendation of the Committee clarifies the political differences in housing policy. I expect the debate here today to do the same.

Housing prices have quadrupled since 1993 and are now higher than ever in Norwegian history. The capital requirement will lead to even greater problems for more young people entering the housing market. The rule can push young people into the rental market and increase speculation there. I think there is a need to lower the equity requirement from 15 to 10 pst. the level before the recent increase. The government has announced that there will be a notice of housing policy. The expectations of the Christian People's Party for the message are clear. We expect that there will be a strategy for increased housing in Norway, so that enough housing will be built to satisfy demand. Young people need to be more able to enter the housing market with a better BSU scheme, for example, and in order for more people to have the opportunity to purchase own-dwelling, the equity requirement must also be lowered, in our view. The Christian People's Party believes that there are enough good descriptions of reality and nostalgic feedback from the government; now there is a need for action, measures and instruments that can improve the situation on the housing market. ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e1316	The school has always been an arena of which politicians have been concerned – an arena in which the political party has engaged, has had strong opinions about and inspired to change and improve. In parallel with the increasing inactivity in society, we also see lifestyle diseases and other factors affecting children’s learning, cognitive capacity and academic performance. The simplest measure for increased physical activity is that all young people start going to school, as represented by Aspaker. This is often more problematic than necessary. ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e1459	Since half the hall has now been on the pulpit and shown to Sogn and Fjordane, it is perhaps natural that I explain what this is all about. The matime at Trudvang school in Sogndal is not like math lessons most, and as we know them. Pults and boards are replaced with matte fat in the school grounds and in nature – a simple concept that combines two good, easy math and physical activity. The starting point is simple: there is a sheet with 80 tasks in the gang table, both splitting and multiplication. The class is divided into layers that each get a laminated note with selected tasks that need to be solved. There are two and two layers on each layer, so they can learn how to work together on the tasks as well, and then they run back and deliver the note to the next layer when the task is solved. It started out as a limited research project, where a group of 4 and 5 classes got an hour of physical activity in the school. After two years, it turned out that these students were in a much better physical form and had less risk of heart and car disease than the elephants who did only regular body training according to their curriculum. The further conclusion is that physical activity in the school has an enormous impact on children's health, the environment at the class stage was significantly better, and the children said that they felt greater well-being at school and had greater tolerance for each other. There was less bullying, and students generally had more positive input into problem and conflict resolution. Children with a different culture, language or other skin colour were also better integrated. Even if all other subjects were cut by 5 pst. To get time for the new hour, students did very well on the national tests as well. This can, of course, all run free copies and obtain without any permission from either government or government. But more research is needed in this area. The high school ball in Sogn and Fjordane, with the skilled researcher Geir Kåre Resaland at the head, has applied to the Research Council for 30 million. kr to one of the largest students of schoolchildren's physical activity ever here in the country. We're talking about a large-scale project where all of the 100 schools in the state will participate. I believe that the results of this research will be able to revolutionize the results of the school, revolutionize the students' health, and organise the school day next time. Let's get the science-based experience in place before we move on. Everything indicates that this is going in the denominator direction. The working party will probably also fix this, so the direction is clear. I think we should take the time we need.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e1581	It is about Christmas, and in the country's schools it is now planned to mark the upcoming festival. From experience, we know that in School-Norway there is some uncertainty about how this can be done without conflicting with the desire for an inclusive school that takes into account students related to different religious and life views. Let me turn back at four years, more specifically to December 5, 2008 and the debate in Odelstinget on a new school purpose paragraph. The current Chairman of the Committee, Ms Søreide, was the case-rapporteur and presented a proposal for a new paragraph of purpose, all the parties could stand fully behind, as the rapporteur put it. I allow myself to quote a little from the post of Eriksen Soreide that time: ‘In the new purpose paragraph, values such as respect for human dignity and nature, freedom of mind, closeness, forgiveness, equivalency and solidarity are central. It is clarified in the purpose paragraph that we are to adopt today, that education in school is to be based on values of Christian heritage and tradition and humanistic heritage and tradition. These values are also expressed in different religions and ways of life and in human rights. We put in the purpose paragraph emphasis on what unites us as a society. The objective paragraph shall not merely give a snapshot of today's society. It's important to know your history and your culture to know who you are. The safer you are on your own anchorage, the more open you are to meet what is new, different and perhaps even challenging for yourself." At the time, the rapporteur stressed that the new purpose paragraph Stortinget adopted was solid and that it would be relevant and practical in the school's work. In addition, the Committee agreed on a clearer emphasis on cooperation between home and school and wanted to make parents visible as an important player, a valuable and close partner to the school. The 2008 debate focused on the fact that the cultural heritage is not a static one. It develops and changes. But a living cultural heritage depends on building on something, building on the notions, thoughts and roots that have shaped it. If it doesn't make sense to talk about cultural heritage. At the same time, it is important that the purpose paragraph specifies values which are not date stamped and which should be as good in the present as in the future. In the debate on a new purpose paragraph, the Right also underlined the importance of preserving the national cultural heritage. In today’s society, a purpose paragraph must both meet the need for a safe value anchoring of the students and stress the opportunities and challenges we have in terms of diversity and pluralism. The increased diversity of society makes new demands to live in mutual respect and tolerance. It's just in this kind of context that it's important to know its history. There's a misunderstanding of tolerance if you're constantly working to deplete your cultural heritage. It only creates increased confusion of value in society. In a pluralistic society, it is important to know one's own identity and belonging, because then one has much better prerequisites for dealing with the new. As surely as it goes against Christmas, we hear about schools that are uncertain as to whether it is compatible with the purpose paragraph and a diverse and multicultural student group to continue the tradition of Christmas service. We've even heard about schools that have replaced church visits and Jesus with rites and Christmas parties to avoid troubling in the salad. This may indicate that there is a need to create greater security and awareness of what the new purpose paragraph actually provides, but also to clarify the limits of what the teaching of the RLE subject can and cannot contain. I have noticed with great satisfaction that the Education Directorate on its website has recently published information that says that the school can carry out religious services as part of its general cultural and traditional communication, while clarifying that this cannot be part of the RLE subject. Furthermore, it is emphasised that schools wishing to arrange or participate in worship should anchor this with their parents. The event must be announced and the school must offer non-discriminatory exemption schemes through equivalent alternative offers. It's positive that the directorate is in the forefront of information. However, the need for such information is a symptom that the new purpose paragraph is not well enough under the skin of the Norwegian school. I have tried to investigate what kind of information about the new purpose paragraph that has been sent to the school, without finding anything in particular. The general part of the curriculum emphasises that the school should train people at once. The right believes that the circulation of value is a central part of this mission. The importance of dissemination based on the basic ideas and values that unite us as societies is about how to equip new generations to provide a secure and conscious foundation for their lives and for their role as fellow human and social citizens in Norway. A school that is insecure in its dissemination and, in the spirit of caution, binds itself may be an undesirable obstacle to students being challenged and also informed of the religious and life-seeing diversity in Norway. Unfortunately, in Norway we experience trends in racism, harassment and stigmatisation of people based on religion. We are constantly getting new examples of how this is expressed in the case of Jews and Muslims. This is unacceptable and requires us as a society to stand up and distance. Studies in Norway have revealed anti-Semitism attitudes which may be difficult to imagine, and therefore the three-year project launched to combat anti-Semitism and racism is very important. We must take a clear distance from attitudes and actions that affect individuals and religious groups in our country. But projects are projects. Therefore, it is so important that the daily work at school is firmly based on the values on which the new purpose paragraph is based. As a shared arena, the school is an important place for bridge construction and for preventing intolerance, which can often be explained by a lack of knowledge of each other's religions, life views and cultural background. This is why the Right believes school owners, school leaders and teachers need to become even more aware of the role that the school can and should play in order to create insight, respect and understanding of how different religions and views of life are practiced in our society. In this way, the school can help lay the foundation for the individual student's formation and equip the students with a positive value set for life. In the 2008 committee resolution, when the school’s new purpose paragraph was adopted, an overall committee, on the one hand, fixed the need for a secure value anchoring for students and, on the other hand, the challenges and opportunities of today’s multicultural society. At the same time, the then Education Committee underlined the importance of the purpose paragraph expressing a set of values that should be able to conclude as far as possible, irrespective of religious, cultural or national ties. Right does not accuse the school, but we want a better follow-up of the school at this point. We believe that a more competent school, which makes it clearer to identify values you can end up with, regardless of religious, cultural or national origin, will be a better school for all students. On the right, the new school’s purpose paragraph is well adapted to a more diverse and multicultural Norway. The school shall, as the Right sees it, also have the opportunity to continue Christian traditions in Norwegian society. But at the same time, it is important to stress that it means that the inclusive school is obliged to devote similar attention to other religious and life-vision festivals and brand days, when it falls naturally. There can be no doubt about Storting's ambitions for the new purpose paragraph, and that it was a demanding road the Norwegian school would go up. Therefore, it is also important that we take an interest in whether practices in school have become what the Stortinge provided, and we need to ask if the school has received the necessary guidance to meet the expectations of the Stortinge. The right considers that there is a basis for saying that the school can become safer in its practice of the purpose paragraph. In a number of areas, useful guides have been developed to support teachers and school leaders. I would therefore like to ask the Council of State whether she will consider equipping Norwegian school with a guide on how the modernised purpose paragraph can be met and practised in the best possible way.

RLE has been part of teacher training to address this need, but, in parallel with increasing school diversity and increasing societal diversity challenges, the subject has been abolished as a compulsory subject in teacher training. The results of this also proved to be pretty fast. At teacher training in Finnmark, RLE is no longer a choice, and at the University of Oslo and Akershus, the number of people taking RLE has been reduced from 320 in 2010 to 80 in 2011. The National Council for Christian Knowledge and Religious Science is deeply concerned about the developments we see. They also point out that there is a disparity between a society that needs a greater knowledge and understanding of religion and outlook than ever before, and that fewer teachers are trained to do so. Signals are clear, and if no action is taken to correct this, we will go into a period of inadequacy in school, which in turn will result in a lack of competence, respect and understanding among the children. It is not difficult to imagine that this can create major challenges, and it is in any case not a worthy follow-up to the building of competence we want for our children. We want children who not only naturally relate to diversity, but who understand and respect the same diversity, and who are safe on their own. It is important to listen to the national technical council, which warns against the negative recruitment of the subject. At the same time, both they and other key actors see opportunities and some positive ones that are important to take with them when the government now needs to address the situation. Many suggest that those who now choose depression in RLE have a greater interest in the subject they have chosen, and thus naturally have a very good starting point for further work. In view of this, it is natural to ask the Council of State how the Department will in a good way stimulate this depression and what strategy the Council of State has to take to ensure such education throughout the country. Alternative courses in the field have also received increased attention. On the basis of this, the Theological Faculty of Society has entered into a partnership with the University of Eastfold on a teaching programme in religion and society and has wanted to establish 20 places of study. They have not been satisfied with funding for this, and since the institution already has very low basic funding from the State, they do not see themselves able to further develop the offer without support for the part they perform in cooperation with the Higher Education College in Eastfold. However, there is no doubt that the education will get applicants, and that this is precisely directed at the lack of knowledge we are now discussing. Will the Council of State specifically follow up with funding for new offers, which can provide us with professionally qualified teachers in the RLE field? We have a good school, we have many good teachers, and during this period we have had great attention to the quality and learning outcomes of education. That's a good lead. It is therefore natural for us in the Right to raise that question also about RLE education. This important area deserves the attention of the government when it comes from a professional perspective, and it is natural for the Storting to challenge the Council of State on what measures can be taken to correct the alarming developments. I therefore look forward to hearing what the Council of State and the Department have been thinking, and to hearing the commitment of the other parties to the Stortinget in this matter.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e1846	The representative Harberg addresses important elements of the school: how knowledge is the basis for multicultural and multireligious coexistence, and the need for new generations through school to gain good religious knowledge. Here I fully agree with Harberg. The importance of knowledge and competence for an inclusive school can hardly be overestimated. St.meld. No 11 for 2008-2009, The Teacher – The role and education, underlines the diversity of students. It is necessary that all teachers have knowledge, skills and attitudes in order to meet this diversity in their teaching and in their subjects. New primary school teachers were started in 2010. The plan for these training courses consists of a framework plan and national guidelines. Here, the leaders of St.meld are made concrete. No 11th@item: Among other things, the plant describes: the learning outcomes for candidates to meet a diverse student mass in a multicultural and multilingual society. In the new elementary school teacher education, the government, in the light of the decision of the Storting, is placing greater freedom of choice on the part of students. Students are far more able to study the subjects in which they are most interested and in which they want to teach. This will help to increase the quality of school education. It is true that RLE is not a compulsory subject in the new teacher training courses, but an elective subject like most other subjects. On the other hand, the new and expanded pedagogical subject, PEL, is mandatory. There are 60 study credits, while general teacher education had 30 study credits. The subject has a very school-relevant and practical content, precisely in order for teacher training to be more effective than before in training good teachers for an inclusive and diverse school. The PEL subject covers themes such as: diversity of pupils development and learning in different social, religious and cultural contexts democracy development of identity mutual tolerance and respect for multicultural understanding, etc. Many of these themes reflect in general the curriculum of the Knowledge Lift. Each student shall have a learning outcomes in all these areas, and the fully-educated elementary school teachers will have this competence in their luggage into the teaching process and into their teaching in all subjects. We have set up a separate follow-up group for primary school teacher training to assist the department with the introduction of the new teacher training courses. This year, the Follow-up Group is working especially with a multicultural perspective. How are the requirements of the framework plan being followed up on institutions? They look in particular at the role of PEL in primary school teacher education. So it's this pedagogical subject, and not the RLE subject, that is to make sure that teachers have the competence to meet and teach the diversity of students. The subject of education and training shall form the basis for all other subjects. I trust that the candidates from primary school teacher education will be better equipped to meet today's diverse student mass than the general teacher was. Therefore, the competence of RLE is not crucial to whether the candidates from the new educations become good teachers for a multicultural and diverse student mass. Of course, the RLE subject must, now as before, take care of the specific knowledge of different religions and views of life. It is important that we have enough candidates with the skills to teach in this subject, as in all other subjects, at school. However, the RLE subject is a small subject in primary school, which amounts to only 7.5 pst. by the hours of the students. The number of RLE qualified teachers should be seen in this respect. Teachers' selection in recent years does not indicate a decline in interest in the RLE subject. The number of teacher students who want to spend at least 30 credits in RLE seems to stay at about the same level as before. The question of the scope of RLE as an opt-out in teacher training must be considered in the light of the requirements for the competence of teachers in school. In June 2012, the Stortinget adopted amendments to the Training Act to introduce requirements on relevant competences in the field of education as of 1 January 2014, in order to help teachers have relevant training in all subjects in which they teach. This comes as an addition to today's skills requirement, which is linked to the employment itself. The specific competence requirements will be regulated in a regulation which will first be consulted. However, the legislative proposal gives signals on the content of the proposed regulation. The content of the rules to be consulted has not been definitively established. The question of whether formal competence — at least 30 study credits — should be required for the teaching of the RLE subject at the youth level is one of the questions raised at the hearing, together with the question of whether a number of other subjects should be subject to competence requirements. I would also like to highlight the employer's responsibility for the school's adequate competence in e.g.: RLE, cf., Training Act § 10-8. School owners and principals must make the needs of the school visible. They must ask for and recruit teachers with the desired professional skills. They are responsible for developing the skills of staff to gain new skills in school. And they have to work to keep the skills that already exist. In the case of new places of study, these are normally awarded either as strategic sites where the institutions themselves prioritise their use, or as education and training areas. For example, neither the Stortinget nor the Ministry of the Interior directs study places for special elective subjects in the following areas: teacher training. Institutions use the study places as they deem it appropriate within the overall framework defined by the Stortinget or the Ministry, and in terms of search and their own professional resources. I don't see any reason to change this.

In addition, NDLA is established for secondary education. Digital learning material is a prerequisite for good learning effect through the use of digital tools. It is therefore also necessary to develop digital learning materials for all subjects at all levels. Digital learning materials are essential for ICT to become an integral part of all learning. This is where the state has a great responsibility. In 2006 it was granted 50 million. kr for digital learning material. It was the county councils that could apply for the funds. All counties, except Oslo, then joined forces to establish a national digital learning arena. In 2007, it was granted under 400 million. to provide free learning material for secondary school students. Some are financed by the counties themselves. If we are to succeed in ICT education, and learners actually get digital skills, we should consider also having educational mid-term bets where schools can assess offers from different providers. Today, the majority of all funds go from the counties to the digital bet in NDLA. This year Akershus district withdrew from the scheme. If there are several states that are withdrawing from the scheme, we do not know as of today, but it is very important to follow this development. Infrastructure in the form of machinery and networks must be in place to enable teachers and students to have a full learning effect. A joint effort by the State, the municipality and the county community should be assessed. Relatively, the challenge is access to equipment in primary schools. In secondary schools, students usually own or rent the equipment themselves. We must be aware of this in the further work. Purchasing and ordering skills should be strengthened, and it must always be ensured that educational purposes govern the purchases to the school. It is important that centres for ICT in education are actively involved in the formulation of the wider policy. ICT and modern technologies provide new opportunities to facilitate more active learning and adapted training for students. But it requires anchoring of use at all associated schools, including students, teachers, school managers, school owners and parents. It also requires teachers to be safe and competent in technology, to experience it as a useful supplement, and to be able to reflect with increasingly skilled students. How will the Council of State facilitate the systematic and competent use of ICT in schools to strengthen the learning of students?

ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e2117	I'm happy to be allowed to speak to the Storting on this theme, which engages me strongly. There is a lot of excitement in ICT education, and some of this I will share with you here today. The use of digital tools was introduced as one of the five basic skills of the Knowledge Lift in 2006. Norway was the first country in Europe to have a curriculum linking digital skills with professional goals. This was a very important step, as we also see clearly identified in the training. The promise of knowledge led to a growth in the interest of ICT in school. The time spent by students with computers in professional settings increased markedly in primary school in the period 2005–2007, and then flattened out. Investments in equipment, continuing training of ICT teachers, use of ICT in the disciplines and training in the safe use of ICT were prioritised. The proportion of teachers who use computers weekly or more frequently has doubled in many subjects, both in primary, secondary and secondary schools since 2005. We know that there are many schools and municipalities that have systematically integrated ICT into the disciplines. We've already got the first lucky high school. Maybe it's just a matter of time before we also get lucky high school. However, the quality is more than the quantity that is crucial when I am satisfied with how well ICT can be integrated into training. The content of school training is governed by the objectives of competence in the curriculum. There is a strong emphasis on teachers and schools having a high degree of freedom in the selection of methods. It is the professional responsibility of schools and teachers to choose the content and working methods that they believe will contribute in the best possible way to the achievement of their objectives. The technology provides opportunities for activisation, concreteisation, motivation, variation and relevance in the subjects. We have to make even better use of this. ICT centres are established and have been given important tasks by the government. This is because we wanted to ensure that we have a knowledge-based policy in the field of ICT, and that we should reach out to the policy sector. Therefore, this centre has a driving role within its field. It must be practical in its approach and explore the educational potential of a new technology. I am satisfied with what the Centre has achieved over the three years it has worked. For example, ICT centres in education have developed around 80 online tools, guides, reports and fact sheets for use in the school sector. The Centre is responsible for a central action in the headlines on youth – Virtual Mathematics School – i.e. a math school online. In this project, the aim is that innovative, interactive and new forms of learning will stimulate students to deliver more and get better results. The project shall target students who need greater challenges than they receive in the ordinary course of training, as well as those who struggle with mathematics. The virtual mathematics school shall be a complement to ordinary education and aim to increase the motivation of the individual student. I was out last night looking at this. We have a network of students who are able to test out this online math education for those students who need major professional challenges while they're in high school. It was so very promising and interesting. There were students who discussed and reflected around mathematics at a very high level, with a teacher located somewhere in the country and student groups elsewhere. When we get this rolled out as an offer across the country, we're actually going to see that there are some possibilities here that are much bigger than many believe today. There are an incredible number of schools and teachers who are good and creative in their use of ICT. The center must work for both the schools and the teachers who are in the middle, the people who are following, and the great mass of schools and teachers who are in the middle. Dissemination of experience is a key word. For this reason, the Centre participates both nationally and internationally to test and disseminate experiences from ground-breaking ways of working, such as:\t\t You can change the settings of the grid. \t\t \t iTEC, Innovative Technologies for an Engaging Classroom, is a four-year project to test out larger and less ICT-based teaching facilities in thousands of European classrooms. Nordic interactive board projects study what happens to the teacher role when interactive boards are used. Space for learning is a modern learning space as an example of the design of learning venues using ICT, where kindergartens, schools and teacher training, in cooperation with the Centre, develop and test the use of ICT in games, learning and teaching. We have some clear challenges. First, there are still schools and teachers who have not taken on sufficiently well that digital skills are necessary for our students to succeed in social and work life. It turns out that students who succeed in using ICT in a good way in school work are the same pupils who succeed in school work in general and have essentially the same social background. This means that teachers need to pay more attention to the qualitatively good use of ICT to prevent the emergence of new digital divisions. Secondly, it is a challenge for digital media to distract students. Teachers must be clear leaders and have a conscious relationship with when, how and why ICT should be used. Class management is an important element of two major initiatives for which the Directorate of Education is responsible: The Youth Strategy, which is a school-based competence development in reading, accounting and classroom management, is the five-year effort for the Better Learning Environment. Class management in classrooms with many computers is not significantly different from a classroom in the traditional sense. It's about supportive relationships, structure, rules, learning culture, motivation and expectations. We need to help teachers who are uncertain about technology by providing good examples and practical tips on how to deal with concrete issues. I have therefore commissioned the Centers for ICT in education to develop online resources with practical tips for good rules and practices in high-tech classrooms. The use of digital tools cannot and must not be seen in isolation from other basic skills such as reading, writing, billing and verbal skills. The use of digital tools is more than technical skills. Critical and reflective use is central. It is also about critical source use and the consequences of irresponsible use. In short: good digital judgment. I stand by the fact that the curriculum and the introduction of digital skills as a basic skill are one of the strongest tools we have to ensure that students have the knowledge they need after finishing school. In the knowledge society, individuals need to be able to see links and further develop their knowledge and competences. Digital skills will be essential to equip each one for the future. Assessment and assessment methods must also be influenced by the content of the curriculum. Digital assessment is about both digital assessment and digital assessment. The school must develop good practice in both the meanings of digital assessment, both in-flight and when to set a position. We already have ICT-based national tests, and the Education Directorate is working with the ICT Centre in education to develop a test in digital skills. Increasing use of the Internet in education has also led to increased demand for Internet use for exams. So we've started an Internet test on exams. The feedback after the first year is positive. I'm very excited about the continuation of this, among other things. because similar tests in Denmark show that there has been a catalyst for the development of the subject and that teaching has been a quality promise. They have received more effective education, better opportunities to take advantage of more of their learning styles and a greater understanding of the breadth and depth of their subjects. The teacher is very important for students’ learning, including in the field of digital skills. This means, of course, that the teacher must also have digital competence. The teacher’s ability and initiative to dare to try out is a good starting point for successful ICT teaching. The teacher must understand and know the arenas in which the students are acting, and the opportunities in the technology and in the digital tools. The educational environment represents a critical factor because they are responsible for basic training and at the same time face most of the provision of continuing training. It has therefore been important for me to highlight those areas of the new framework plan and the national guidelines for primary education. There is a lot of exciting happening in relation to the use of ICT in education. It is linked not only to the fast pace of technology development but also to the way we think about education. In this area, there are many examples, as the interpellant also had, of how to turn the way you work in the classroom using new technologies.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e2356	Children in flight mirror a profoundly unfair world. When people flee, children are hit harder than adults. An overall committee welcomes the government's initiative for a wide-ranging review of the situation of these children. The main message is about children seeking protection in Norway, alone or with adults. However, it is also about preventing children from travelling or being sent on a dangerous journey towards Europe and Norway without a need for protection. The message describes the international picture and the extent of children in flight. It describes en route abuse, that some just disappear, cynical traffickers and traffickers, Norway's international work for children are also addressed, including improving the conditions of country children fleeing from, and our support to organisations working for the protection of children globally. A separate chapter is devoted to children in refugee camps. These are the children who come to Norway and seek protection, who are most engaged in the public debate here at home. International Conventions and Agreements allocate responsibility for providing protection. We have previously addressed a communication on migration in a European perspective. An overall increase was therefore in favour of harmonisation of legislation and common minimum standards for the treatment of asylum applications and asylum seekers. It's important, not least for the children. Some 10,000 people apply for asylum in Norway each year, but the figure has varied considerably, with a preliminary peak in 2009 of just over 17 000 applicants. Last year there were 9,053. Nevertheless, the proportion of children is quite stable at around 30 pst. More children come together with adults than alone. About 70 pst. of the lone children. For accompanying children, the proportion is about half of this. Children in need of protection are given refugee status and residence in Norway. But many also come without protection, hoping to get a better future. Some of them are allowed to stay on the basis of strong human considerations, but there are relatively few. The public debate on refugee children often revolves around children who are neither entitled to protection nor allowed to stay on a humanitarian basis. The Committee organised a case-by-case consultation as part of its examination of the notification. Twelve agencies signed up — mainly non-governmental organisations working with children, but also others. We got a lot of input and concrete suggestions, which are listed and commented on in our attitude. Here, as in the other comments of the Committee, there is a relatively broad consensus in the Grand Duchy on government policy in this area, but there are some shades ranging from the desire for a slightly tighter line to the desire for clarification and a certain decoration of the legislation in the form of regulatory changes. Some of this is concrete in the form of seven minority proposals, one from the Right and six from the Christian People's Party. I assume that they themselves take up and justify their proposals. In ten minutes there will be no time to address all the issues in the message and from the hearing, but other speakers will go into some of them. I want to spend a little time on the subject of long-term children, which is thus a major feature of the public debate. There are issues where children and/or parents do not receive protection under international conventions. A new assessment is then made whether they may still be due to strong human considerations. The child’s best course is then weighed against immigration-regulating considerations, and a few more are allowed to stay. The best of the child, as one of several fundamental considerations, then becomes decisive. When this balance also gives rise to a refusal, the problem may arise for a long time, but not always. Some comply with the decision and return voluntarily within the time limit. They have access to relatively good return programmes, which the Communication also proposes to strengthen by DKK 10 000 per child. Last year, over 300 children returned voluntarily, almost all following adults. Approximately twice the number was forced to return – approx. One-fourth of them were lone minors. Most of them were returned to another European country. Both children and adults are obliged to leave at the time of final rejection. Some extremely few cannot return voluntarily, but they may be. Others, who do not travel on a voluntary basis, are initially to be transported out, but this is often difficult. There may be capacity problems in the police, some countries refuse to accept their own citizens on forced return, some applicants refrain from returning, going into hiding, or refusing to cooperate in clarifying their identity. The result is that some children stay for a long time in Norway, without a residence permit. Some were born in Norway after their parents came here. These are the long-term children. They are, or become, Norwegian in mind and skin. The number is estimated to be around 450, depending on what one rains as long as it lasts. In 2007, the Government introduced a provision, §8 to 5 of the Foreign Regulation, that children’s relationship with Norway should be given particular attention when assessing when a residence permit should be granted on the basis of strong human considerations. In its treatment of new foreign law, which entered into force on 1 January 2010, the majority stressed that the greater the importance of the decision for the child, the more it should be in order to focus on immigration policy considerations, and the importance of the child’s best interests in certain contexts would be so heavy that access to other considerations is not essential. In 2011, the Government adopted a further amendment to the Foreign Regulation, which specifies that child considerations are to be set out separately in the decisions. In the Storting hearing, several organisations considered it difficult to see from the decisions how the best of the child was weighed against other considerations. The Committee has therefore chosen to stress the importance of the drafting of the decisions in a way which makes them verifiable. Organisations also asked for a clearer clarification of the factors to be included in the child’s best judgements and that, according to them, a clarification of the expected practice should take the form of a regulatory provision stating how the child’s best interests should be assessed and the weight to be attached to that consideration. The majority of the Committee, like the government, considers that the current legislation is really good enough, but that, in the light of practice, there is a need for clarification. The Communication states that recent UNE practices indicate that in some cases more emphasis is placed on immigration-regulating considerations than is provided for in the legislation. At the same time, practices appear to be less unambiguous. All matters shall be assessed individually, but it shall be considered whether the child has attended kindergarten/school, whether the child speaks Norwegian, participates in leisure activities, is particularly related to the Norwegian community, inter alia. through leisure activities, languages and friends. Residence time with which asylum seekers count. It shall also provide for residence by final decision. UNE today chooses to ignore this last one. Time limit exceeded is less burdensome than active countermeasures to return, violation of criminal law, etc. However, it is clarified that at some point the best interests of the child will have to be given priority in any case. Health problems, treatment options in the home country, the need for stability and tranquillity of children, the situation of care in return, trafficking in human beings and the risk of sexual mutilation are also factors to be considered. The notification and the treatment of the Storting are to be seen as a follow-up to the new Foreign Affairs Act, which clarifies and clarifies the intentions of the legislator. It is assumed that the non-resident authorities will act accordingly. Policy shall be reviewed at regular intervals below. The first review we get in the summer of 2013. However, the most important thing — in any case — must be to prevent children from getting into such a vacuum, as many of the long-term children do. We do this best through faster procedures, including I would like to mention the new 15-month deadline for proceedings in UNE, which has now been at the hearing, as well as faster returns. All these measures will also allow us to discuss further in the context of the 2013 budget, because the government will follow up what is stated in the communication, with concrete actions and appropriations.
