ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e2422	I would first like to thank the case rapporteur for a good factual description of the matter. As the rapporteur also pointed out, there is a relatively large consensus around most of the principles in relation to the notification of children on the run. We all agree that children are a particularly vulnerable group, and especially children who are in a refugee situation. It's also interesting to ask yourself the question in this context: what is a child really? Children are often defined to be anyone under the age of 18. This involves teenagers, or — to put it bluntly — young people who are referred to as children. Most of the young people I know would be powerfully provoked if I appealed to them as a child – even though they are far from being considered adults. Age can be a lot, and science operates with more age than the purely chronological age, i.e. How many years we've lived. Mental age indicates the level of maturity, and physical age indicates the level of physical development of the human being. Many of those referred to as children in immigration policy show a physical and mental maturity that should lead to adult age. Another challenge we face is the actual age of asylum seekers in relation to what they are informed by themselves. This is a matter that one has to consider when debating children on a refugee basis. In Report. Number 27 for 2011–2012 is divided into two main groups. One group is children who accompany adults seeking asylum in Norway. For me, it's natural that children accompany adults. As a result, families will not be split regardless of the outcome of an asylum application. This is precisely a matter of little controversy, but some people believe that long periods of residence in Norway should give the right to a residence permit, which is an opinion that I did not sign. There is always a reason why the processing of an asylum application takes a long time. The most common reason is that unclear or incorrect information has been provided that may take a long time to find out. I see no reason for this to be premiered. It is therefore important that measures are taken to reduce the duration of proceedings. No one, either asylum seekers or the Norwegian community, has been served with a long trial period. If they remain, even if they remain illegal, they establish ties, which are again used as arguments in new requests. This means that the undersigned is mistaken. Nor do I see any reason why it is to be presented that a person will not comply with the legitimate decisions of the appropriate authorities, and that instead of returning to the home country as provided for, a person chooses to train the decision by making new requests for conversion. The second group is the lone minor asylum seekers. I have little faith that all lone minor asylum seekers are actually lonely. This I think is just a disappearing little minority. On the contrary, they demonstrate the fact that they have travelled to Norway and the fact that they are applying for asylum, precisely that they have a social network. In Report. St. 27 has not published statistics on the country of origin of the lone minor asylum seekers, but countries such as Afghanistan are also Iraq. It is also referred to the Horn of Africa – which must be a rewriting for Somalia. What characterises these three countries is a relatively low wage level as well as high unemployment. It is unlikely that a young asylum seeker from some of these countries has managed to finance the trip to Norway. This means that someone other than the asylum seeker himself has paid the trip for this youth or this child. This also shows that the asylum seeker has a network, at least in the home country, and in many cases also in Norway. However, it also shows that some people are trying to take advantage of the situation of minor asylum seekers or minors from countries that do not have it as well as we do in Norway. So there's someone who makes money to take advantage of children in this case as well. From this point of view, it is not entirely consistent with the facts when the asylum seeker is referred to as a single person. The network is there, even though it is a criminal network. In many cases, there is doubt about both age and identity. Both of these matters speak in favour of the asylum seeker. It is justified to ask questions as to what are the real reasons why the asylum seeker does not want to appear with his right identity. This applies to adult asylum seekers, but of course it also applies to minors. I have recorded that there has been criticism of physical testing of age, but there is a lot of evidence that the X-rays currently carried out by teeth and wrists are good enough. I notice that the number of asylum applications from single minors fell dramatically after tests were carried out consistently. I would like to conclude that thorough tests to detect age must be maintained, while priority must be given to applications from the group of lone minor asylum seekers and they must be given high priority. It is important that those who are rejected be returned as soon as possible. A housing situation, a housing situation, in an asylum recipient is not good — and at least not for young people. At the same time, there must be strict practice in granting or refusing asylum applications. What is at least equally important is that once an asylum application has been granted, a few people have to quickly get into a normal housing situation in a municipality. I understand that municipalities refuse to accept asylum-seekers – or rather, former asylum-seekers who have been granted residence – when they see how the economy of these municipalities is and what kind of economy comes with those who have been granted a residence permit. It is important especially, as I said, for children to get into a normal living situation, but it is also important that they get a housing situation that is as good as possible in the municipalities they come to. There has also been a lot of talk about the best of children and what is best for children in a situation for asylum seekers. Some people use the argument that the best thing for a child will always be to get his application accepted. I'm sure that's right. When considering which countries they come from, it will always be better in Norway than in the country from which they come. But in my opinion, the best thing for a child is to be close to his or her own family, to be close to his or her parents, as long as these don't abuse the children. It is always the best of children, and therefore one should also seek to achieve the goal of parents and children being as close as possible to each other, so that one does not separate them. As I said, it will always be best in Norway. But I think that children always want the best with their parents, and I think that one has to base it on both the granting and refusal of asylum applications where children are involved.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e2501	First of all, I would like to thank the rapporteur, Lise Christoffersen, who has usually done a very thorough job. The reasons for children leaving their country of origin for applying for protection are several. Some children and families leave their country of origin for economic and social reasons, without a real need for protection under international conventions. They shall initially refuse their application for a stay in Norway and return, but strong human considerations may nevertheless speak for them to remain. At the same time, it must be an objective that practices do not invite others – and especially children – to make a dangerous journey to Norway without any need for protection. In decisions taken between 2005 and 2011, about 60 pst. of children admitted to stay in Norway. The share of concessions was 50 pst, respectively. for accompanying children and 82 pst. for lone minors. Since the entry into force of the new foreign law on 1 January 2010, the percentage of the total granted to the accompanying children has been about 34 p.m. and between 70 and 75 pst. for lone minors. The report shows that the proportion of granted residence permits is highest for lone children and higher for adults with children than for adults without children. Such figures may be due to the fact that the threshold of residence based on strong human considerations is lower for children than for adults and that the best interests of the child have been taken into account in some cases, although there is initially no need for protection under international rules. At the same time, the high proportion of lone minors granted protection indicates that child-specific conditions are given priority in the protection assessment. In the period 2005–2011, 944 voluntary children were returned, almost all of whom were accompanying children, in cooperation with the International Organization for Migration, IOM. In the period 2009–2011, 1780 children were transported out by the Police Foreign Unit, of which 1460 were accompanying children. 319 of those transported had declared themselves to be lone minors on arrival in Norway. A majority of the latter were returned under the Dublin II Regulation and their main weight was registered as being over 18 years in the European country to which they were returned. In 2010, five single minors were repatriated to their home country after processing the asylum case in Norway. To the right, the broad commitment is valued for children who are not allowed to stay in Norway, as we have seen from individuals and non-governmental organisations. This is an important contribution to the ongoing assessment of whether Norwegian legislation and practices are consistent with national objectives and international obligations regarding children’s rights. Refugee children are a comprehensive theme. The notification covers all children seeking protection in Norway, not only legal refugees. We then stressed that Norwegian refugee and migration policies are determined by the Norwegian political authorities in accordance with Norway's international obligations and agreements, that, in order to achieve the government's overall objective of a humane, solidarity and legal certainty policy on asylum and refugees, closer cooperation with other European countries is necessary through harmonisation of laws and regulations, but that this does not limit our freedom to adopt national standards. In the public debate on refugee children, the focus is particularly on long-term children. An important element of this debate has been how the commitment of Norway under the UN Convention on the Rights of the Child to be a fundamental consideration in all kinds of matters relating to children should be understood and practised in the treatment of applications for asylum involving children. This issue is particularly relevant in the context of requests for conversion. ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e2578	Thank you to the rapporteur for very solid work, to the major fraction for demanding and important debates and to the Committee for a clean process. So it's Report. St. 27 for 2011–2012, Children on the run, we are discussing today. For us in SV, this is actually a brand day, because the message addresses very central issues related to children in flight and states that there is a need for changes. The main message in the message is that greater emphasis should be given to the best interests of children. Ensuring basic rights for children in Norway is an important political objective for the government, and it is in line with Norway’s international obligations, such as: consequences of the Children’s Convention. It is also – and it is good to note – in line with the main message from all the hearing bodies. I would like to take advantage of the opportunity to make special mention of six important points in the message. First, the message explicitly states that the consideration of the best interests of children should be a fundamental consideration in the assessment of the humanitarian status of foreign administrations. The child’s relationship with Norway shall be particularly emphasised in that assessment, read on page 51, and Chapter 6 contains assessments, reflections or considerations on what this should mean and what it means in practice. Secondly, the message points out that foreign students – i.e. The UNEs practice has become progressively more stringent, and the government notes that UNE has placed too much emphasis on immigration-regulating considerations in some cases. In my opinion, this is a big message at its best. One puts the searchlight on practice and concludes that practice needs to be improved and points to how it should be improved. Thirdly, the message confirms that illegal residence is a less burdensome immigration regulatory consideration. This means that there is less room from now on to reject applications on the basis of illegal residence in cases where children have a strong connection with the kingdom, and it is best for children to stay in Norway. A fourth important measure by the government is to ensure good child-care skills at all levels of the foreign administration. This is important when we look at it in the context of the ambitions of the legislation and of this big message: we need to take more and not least better account of the best interests of children, because we want a better and wiser child-discipline exercise. It requires capacity at all stages, but not least competence, both in child-care issues and not least in how to get young people to participate in such complex processes that we are talking about here. The fifth thing I want to draw out is that the living conditions of children in reception are to be regularly monitored and evaluated. Many of us noticed that the Christian Tybring-Gjed of the Advance Party claimed in the NRK opinion a few weeks ago that at these receptions everything was kind of very well and the week money was very much so rusty. Fortunately, he did not get so much support for it. On the contrary, there are many who have filed the opposite view and the opposite suspicion – fine that we should now have a review. Finally, the message – I have already mentioned it – underlines that the right of young people to be heard should be an important part of the asylum process – it is to hear young people directly, not just indirectly through their representation. This is about taking care of a democratic fundamental right, which I think we appreciate more and more in all the contexts of our society, but not least it is a contribution to getting more quality into the process – so get the right decisions than we might have today. So here's a clear message. I would like to underline and appreciate the fact that Lise Christoffersen, the case rapporteur, operates with a very precise and important concept of the post-work of the law. That's exactly what this is all about. It is not necessary to amend a law or regulation, but it is necessary, through the post-work of the law, to prepare a post-work into the law just to clarify what is the intention of the law. So I don't think we're going to overdramatize that we've had a practice development that's gone the wrong way. It is not easy for a government to steer by political signals, and there is no defeat necessarily for that administration. But the more important and correct it must be – both for the administration we are talking about here, and not least for us who are responsible for the political signals to be given – that we are just getting to the clean-up that we are doing here, and giving a clear direction in the future for how the administration will operate here. In this respect, I would urge all the young people who have now received a final rejection – well 500 pieces – who have lived for a long time in Norwegian asylum recipients to promote a change request, so that we can clean this up.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e2652	Asylum policy is a complex policy area, with difficult dilemmas between the consideration of individuals in vulnerable situations and the consideration of a consistent, coherent and fair policy. These balances become extremely difficult for children, whether they come alone or together with family. Regardless of the background, children’s migrants will be in a particularly vulnerable situation, and therefore extra attention should be paid to these cases. This applies from the start of the search process and until the child may have been allowed to stay and be integrated into the Norwegian community. Additional consideration must also be given to children in cases where they are the subject of a refusal and shall return either on their own or with family or other caregivers. In recent years, the proportion of children seeking protection in Norway has been below 30 pst. of the total number of asylum seekers. For various reasons, many of these children and their families have been in a situation they experience that is unexplained for too long. Although in many cases this is due to the fact that they have not even returned after final rejection, it will in any case be a burden for children to have their childhood put on hold. The reception of asylum is not a good framework for a child's growth. It is good that we have now received a big message specifically for this group, which contributes to an overall approach to both legislation, practice and policy. Human dignity, humanistic traditions and the international work of Norway to help persecuted people underpin the Central Party's asylum and refugee policy. We must pursue a humane, solidarity and legal certainty of asylum and refugee policy towards those seeking protection in Norway. At the same time, as a joint committee does, we must recognise that increasing migration cannot solve the problems created by an unfair distribution of world resources. International efforts to help as much as possible close to where they live therefore need to be strengthened. The Committee has agreed that it is a priority task to prevent lonely children from making dangerous travels without need of protection, where they are often vacated organised criminal groups smuggling people for economic profit. Refugee children are a comprehensive theme where an overall approach is needed. I would like to summarise the measures and priorities in three main areas: they will prevent children from leaving their home country without need of protection; they will protect vulnerable children in Norway; and they will prevent children from staying for a long time in an unexplained situation. Regardless of residence status, it is important that children and young people offer meaningful activities. In the case of single minors with temporary authorisations and for those who have received a final refusal and are waiting for a return, the offer should be geared to the knowledge they can directly benefit from when returning to the country of origin. This could be done, for example, by: relate to language skills or practical skills. In the public debate on refugee children, the focus is particularly on long-term children. The interpretation of the UN Children’s Convention that the best interests of children should be a fundamental concern in all kinds of matters relating to children is central and has been discussed. In the Communication, the Government considers that recent practice in dealing with complaints by the Department of Foreign Affairs suggests that, in some cases, more emphasis is placed on immigration policy considerations than was foreseen at the time of the adoption of the legislation. The message we are examining today highlights the emphasis of key elements in the assessment to be made by the best interests of children. Of the factors to be assessed, the notification refers to age, total length of residence in Norway, whether the child has attended school or kindergarten, whether it speaks Norwegian, whether it is taking part in leisure activities, or whether it is otherwise specifically related to Norwegian society. In particular, it is important to prevent children from coming into such a situation that they remain in reception for a long time. The notification focuses on shorter processing times, faster return and faster residence of those who are allowed to stay. The red-green government has strengthened the rights of children in foreign legislation and further strengthens their position through the current communication. Nevertheless, it will still be the case that some, including families with children, are not granted asylum in Norway. In this case, it is because the Norwegian foreign authorities in several instances have considered it safe for them to return, and there are also no strong human considerations that suggest that they should be. In other cases, taking into account the best interests of the child will be so burdensome that the child may remain in Norway, although it may be safe to return. The notification clarifies how the provision is to be understood.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e2675	I want to start with a quote: "The way I go is just until I turn 18, then I don't know. Then I don't have a way." This said a lone minor asylum seeker aged 16 years to Red Barna when they traveled around to hear what children were doing on asylum reception. 149 unaccompanied minor asylum seekers are currently sitting around the country and grunting until they turn 18, because the lone minor asylum seekers also receive a birthday present on the 18th birthday. The gift is from the world's best country to live in, but is neither a driver's license, nor a car, nor a grand celebration. The gift is a letter where with a single sentence it is black on white that they must now leave the country. Leave the best country in the world. Abandoned friends. Leave a life of safety and enter a life alone, consisting of uncertainty and fear. This is the result of the government introducing in the autumn of 2008 a number of restrictive measures in the field of asylum policy. Among these measures was the introduction of what were known as limited permits without the right to renewal for lone minor asylum seekers over 16 years of age, a system that allows children between 16 and 18 years of age to stay only until they reach 18 years of age, then to be thrown out of the country – all in order to save money. This is a practice that requires these young people to live unpredictablely, with life waiting. Norway treats these young people in a way that affects them, among other things. their schooling, social life and mental health — everything to save money. Studies show that many of these young people live with severe mental disorders, not only because of previous traumas in their home country and experiences during their flight, but also because of the difficult situation in their lives in which they live with limited permits in Norway. This happens with the richest government of the time, which is going to save money. The Christian People’s Party believes that we simply cannot maintain a system that has caused many young people unnecessary and severe suffering. Therefore, the Christian People's Party today proposes that limited residence permits should no longer be granted. I hope that the government, if they do not support it today, will listen anyway. I would also like to say something about the message's treatment of long-term asylum children. The government says that immigration policy is not being practised as intended. The best interests of children are not put ahead of immigration-regulating considerations, as intended. The question that arises is as follows: Does the government not know the consequences of their own policies, does it not have control over their administration, or will they not now acknowledge the consequences of their policies? The government responds with disclaimer of liability and relaxes responsibility to the non-resident administration. The government owes it to the government not to follow the government's intentions. On the other hand, the Foreign Service states that if the government wants a change, the government must make a regulation. I need to address a further breach of promises from the current government: both Soria Moria I and Soria Moria II state that care for lone minor asylum seekers between 15 and 18 years of age would be transferred to child protection. This is an invaluable measure to prevent the disappearance of lone minor asylum seekers, while providing a better and more comprehensive offer for this group. This is also recommended and pointed out by the United Nations Children’s Committee. It is therefore disappointing that this is still being done, and that children do not receive the offer they have been promised. The elevators are black on white, but they are no more valuable than an air lock – a Soria Moria. I should have liked to talk longer, but rather come back later in the debate, because the message addresses many important issues. The praise is to be given to the government: the message is not only about long-term asylum children or temporary stays, but also addresses very many other important issues on which it is important that we focus. For so long, I hesitate to take up the suggestions that the Christian People's Party has made in this setting. ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e2816	Today, millions of people are fleeing or displaced in the world. Very many of these are children who have left their homeland, either with the family or alone. Many people have Europe as their goal. This is a very risky journey. Some people reach Europe’s asylum systems, while an unknown number gets stuck in unworthy living conditions or loses life along the way. At the same time, therefore, we need to ensure protection for the children who need it and to pursue a policy that does not encourage others to embark on a dangerous journey towards Europe. The best interests of the child are central. The notification makes clear which areas the government wants to prioritise and highlights multi-purpose actions: they must prevent children from travelling from their home country without need of protection, protect vulnerable children in Norway and prevent children from staying for a long time in an unexplained situation. Children coming to Norway alone are particularly vulnerable. There has been a recent public discussion about single minors who receive temporary permits pending their return. The protection needs of children are recognised, but we must assume that many lone minor asylum seekers have not left their home country because they have been persecuted. At the same time, the return of lone minors is very demanding, as caregivers are difficult to track down without the child’s or the child’s family being involved. For the sake of children, we must pursue a policy that does not give parents an incentive to send their children to Europe. In my view, it is an important measure to grant a temporary permit to young people over 16 years of age, pending their return at the age of 18, when they have no other basis for staying in Norway than that we do not know their care provider in their home country. During this period, however, we need to ensure that these young people are taken good care of. In accordance with the recommendation of the United Nations Children’s Committee to equip the children with the skills and skills they will need if they return to their home country, we have created a specific training programme for this group. Today, many children have lived for a long time in Norway, without having received either them or their families a residence permit. These issues raise difficult dilemmas, particularly in relation to the balance against immigration-regulating considerations. We will initiate an external evaluation of how children’s situation is highlighted in the appeal proceedings. I have noticed that the Committee also wants information on the family immigration legislation of protected persons to be included in this evaluation. I agree that this is an important aspect to consider, and believe that this can be made more useful if it is kept separate from this evaluation. It is important to give children a meaningful everyday life in reception. I have therefore invited responsible sectoral authorities and humanitarian organisations to contribute to this. In order to ensure that children do not remain for a long time in Norway without a residence permit, it is essential that asylum applications are treated promptly, as well as that return takes place quickly after rejection. The government has therefore taken action to ensure a faster procedure for families with children, and we have strengthened the return work. At the same time, the government has made clear the current provision for residence due to the relationship of children with Norway. I assume that this is clarifying the work of the foreign agencies, also in a way that can benefit individuals. My wish with the message has been to contribute to a knowledge-based debate on the major dilemmas that children are facing. I am therefore pleased that the communication has aroused interest in the UN High Commissioner for Refugees as well. I myself recently met the UNHCR’s Nordic representative, and I note that also in Geneva it is said that Norway has taken an important initiative with the message to get the search light on the alarming conditions we see in relation to today’s international children’s emigration.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e3060	From 2007 until the end of 2009, there was a sharp increase in the number of asylum seekers who reported being a single minor, which resulted in Norway being the second most single-year-old asylum seeker in Europe in 2009. We went from 403 asylum seekers in 2007. We had 1,374 in 2008, and in 2009 we reached the peak of 2,500 lone minor asylum seekers. There was no sustainable situation. The increase in arrivals resulted in high costs of reception, processing, etc. In 2009, Norway spent more than 1.3 billion. kr only in reception and care centres for lone minor asylum seekers. In addition, the challenge was to deal with the settlement of the residents. This raises issues about Norway in a broader refugee policy perspective, using disproportionate resources to assist a limited group that usually does not come from the most deprived families in the country of origin, many of whom left their home country for reasons other than having a real need for protection. We believe that it would be irresponsible not to focus on the knowledge that has gradually been gained about the dangers to which lone minor asylum seekers are exposed on their journey to Norway. It also appears that reasons other than the need for protection may be the basis for parents to send their children out on their own on a journey to western countries. The possibility of a permanent stay in Norway may result in more children who do not need protection being sent away from family and networks to travel to Norway. In this context, it is also pointed out that the need to protect children from being in a burdensome and serious situation is one of the reasons why, under the Supreme Court, restrictions on children’s rights may be justified in the context of international law. Based on the overall picture of the situation, the Norwegian authorities found it necessary to introduce restrictive measures targeting this group of minors, who are close to the age of authority and who are not found to have a real need for protection. The new rules introduced mean that single minor asylum seekers who have reached the age of 16 at the time of decision may be granted a limited residence permit up to the age of 18 if there are no reasons for residence other than that the Norwegian authorities consider that the child is without proper care upon return. There is always a concrete and individual assessment of whether lone minor asylum seekers are at risk of persecution on return to their home country, or whether the conditions for humanitarian residence are met, in line with the legislation of the Foreign Affairs Act and the international rules of which Norway is bound, inter alia. The Children's Convention. I would like to stress that all single minors in need of protection are to be granted a residence permit in Norway. Without it being possible to identify the direct link between the measures implemented and the development of arrival figures, the statistics show that the number of lone minor asylum seekers fell sharply in 2010. The total number in 2011 is about the same level as in 2010. A total of 30 minors between 16 and 18 years of age were granted such limited authorisation in 2011 by the UDI. In 2010, 41 such permits were granted, and 33 minors were granted such permits in 2009. The granting of a limited authorisation instead of a refusal may create expectation of further residence, so that it does not necessarily justify a voluntary return after the expiry of the authorisation. It may therefore be important for children to receive an offer during the temporary stay, which is truly qualified for return to their country of origin after the age of 18. A good offer could also counteract other negative effects of temporary stay. The Christian People's Party has a proposal where one will remove access to limited permits, and I will join in the considerations that the representative of Tetzschner had, linked to it. I might want to rephrase myself a little bit differently, but the real content I think we share our views on. There are two options if we take this option away. One is that everyone should be allowed to stay. It will have a huge pull effect by issuing a blank authorization. We can't accept that – it won't be manageable. The second option is to return children – to return children without knowing that someone can accept them. This issue is full of dilemmas and, in the light of the different considerations that we have discussed today and which the communication highlights, we believe that the limited authorisation system is better than the alternatives. It's a system I, like the Council of State, want to hold on and defend. We are not blind to the fact that it is not unfavorably positive, but, as I said, in the absence of better alternatives, we believe that this is the best.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e3079	I am pleased that the government has put this matter on the agenda and that we are now having a wide-ranging debate on the situation of the children who are fleeing and who are staying in Norway. It allows us to fix what is not good enough for these kids. This occupies me greatly. I agree with those who say that everything we are going to do in this matter must be the first goal of being at the best of the children. The worst thing we can do to these children is separate them from their parents and siblings. For those who have lost their parents, a safe community, good friends, and a future that feels safe, means everything in order to move on. These children often see no meaning in life when they are torn from an environment where they have finally found security. I myself live in a municipality with few inhabitants, but where we have a great reception for asylum. Those living on this reception are a natural and important part of the building. In particular, children have slipped well into the local environment. These are children who have often lived their entire lives in an asylum reception. These children have no other home country, and they have no other community — or friends — than what they find in this community environment. We also have to look at the situation of parents. There are good reasons to believe that parents’ ability to handle their children’s care is closely linked to what they have experienced in their home country experiences and on their journey to Norway. These are people who are generally tired, but they often change when they have fallen to rest in an asylum reception and when they feel the security of a community. I've been talking to people who work on asylum receptions, who do an amazing job every day. They say that the parents again feel discouraged and become worn out when they start to fear that the broadcast will stand by the door. Of course, the future of children is what makes parents most concerned. Employees in the reception office at my home office say that the reception has a very good relationship with the school. Almost the same families with children of school age arrive at the reception, the school is able to offer a good educational offer. Young people are also included in the social events taking place in the class in a short time. The same thing is true of kindergarten. It is pointed out that disabled children get a very good development when they start in kindergarten – I am very happy to hear that. I also know people in Oslo who have lived here for years, but who still don't know if they can stay here on a permanent basis. These are people who have slipped into a community, who have a job, who pay their taxes – like all of us. These are people who have children born in Norway, who go to kindergarten and school, and who are Norwegian – as much as the rest of us. I understand that these children are very afraid to have to leave Norway. This is like sending out any other Norwegian citizen from their country of birth, or their country of origin. This is an uncertainty that no child in our country will have to live with for a long time.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e3143	The Communication points out that children are particularly vulnerable to being victims of trafficking in human beings in the context of migration. Children can become dependent on human traffickers or other adults who can provide a basis for exploitation in human trafficking. We also know that some children are being exploited by their carers. The government has a broad perspective in the fight against trafficking in human beings, and it is enshrined in government action plans. Children are dedicated to a separate chapter of the government action plan on trafficking in human beings for 2011–2014 because this group of victims is particularly vulnerable and has a special need for protection. In particular, it focuses on children who are found in a drug and criminal environment, and children who disappear from the care centres or reception centres of child protection. Norway is doing a good job in this area and is at the forefront of a broad international effort. Work on trafficking in human beings has been further strengthened by the Council of Europe Convention against Trafficking in Human Beings, which Norway ratified in 2008. It contains a number of provisions relating to children. Victims who are presumed to be children shall be subject to special protective measures until the age of the victim has been brought to justice and the child shall be appointed a representative to take care of his/her interests. Furthermore, the State has obligations to provide for the necessary measures to identify the child and to make every effort to track down the child’s family when it is in the best interests of the child. Victims who are children shall not be returned to a country where a risk and safety assessment indicates that such return would not be in the best interests of the child. Often, police, asylum recipients, health services or outreach establishments first come into contact with children who may be exposed to trafficking in human beings. They are obliged to notify the local child protection services if there is reason to believe that the child is being abused or there are other forms of serious care failure. In Bergen, the police are working well with this field and these matters, and in the summer, six Romanians were sentenced to a total of 14 years and 10 months for gross trafficking in human beings between 13 and 17 years of age. This shows that work is beneficial, but it must have a focus, and it must be targeted against trafficking in human beings. The size of the population has recently decided to introduce a separate legal basis into the Children’s Act to protect minors from trafficking in human beings. The amendment implies that protective measures may be implemented without the consent of the parties. Therefore, in order to ensure the legal certainty of children, clear and strict conditions are laid down for the measures. The government has granted $17 million. to build an organised institutional offer for this vulnerable child group.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e3372	I have started many debates on foreign affairs, saying that there is a major difference in refugee and asylum policy between the Progress Party on the one hand and the other political parties on the other, and so on in this case as well. On behalf of the Workers' Party, I fully support what the case rapporteur said. Everyone agrees that the number of requests for conversion is high, and that it is a serious challenge, but only the Progress Party is willing to set aside international conventions and people's legal certainty to reduce the number of requests for conversion. The other parties are looking for solutions within the international legislation that we are obliged to do. The Council of State also writes in its reply that the high number of requests for conversion was part of the basis for the so-called Board of Appeal to be set up in due time to look at the organisation of appeal proceedings and the organisation of the body itself. The Progress Party will have a total ban on the overall demands for conversion. Of course, it's not possible and has to be viewed as a relatively populist play. The party of progress also claims relatively blatantly that the number of requests for conversion is a symptom of outrightness in the foreign administration. I believe that such expressions do not really apply, and there is hardly any evidence for that either. At final rejection, people usually get a place between seven and thirty days to leave. The objective is, of course, to travel as much as possible within that deadline and preferably voluntarily. The government has done a lot to streamline the foreign administration. More resources have been allocated to the proceedings. The EFFEKT programme has received large amounts of funding over several years, so that the process can be carried out digitally. It has once again opened up a more effective collection of information from other public bodies and a greater certainty that the information in these cases is correct and up-to-date when decisions are taken. The Foreign Affairs Unit of the police is strengthened by increased means of transport. But the most important of all is maybe good return agreements, which give people the opportunity to a new start in the country of origin. On the other hand, it is a problem that some countries do not accept their own citizens by repatriation. The government has therefore just stepped up its efforts to conclude return agreements with the countries of origin. From 5 such agreements in 2005 we now have almost 30. Then, of course, it's also a problem if he doesn't cooperate to establish his identity. That's why there's a national ID center built to strengthen the work of finding out who people are or are not. It's an important job, because where should someone send people if their identity is unknown? With regard to the problem with a high number of requests for conversion, there are no quick and simple solutions. Instead, a job, as the government does, needs to be systematic and long-term in order to reduce the length of proceedings both in the first instance and in the second instance, and to ensure rapid returns. The shorter it takes from refusal to return, the less likely it is that new elements come into the matter. The solution is certainly not to weaken people’s legal certainty by breaking Norway’s international obligations. Today, Norway is a serious partner in European cooperation on asylum issues. We will not continue to be that if we implement unilateral measures that violate the international commitments we have.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e3393	The Progress Party is not interested in breaking international commitments and conventions. We want us to be in line with them, but it is possible to do as other countries have done, to use those conventions and those agreements to get faster treatment and less opportunities for conversion requests. Applicants for asylum in Norway are entitled to legal certainty. It was just supposed to be missing. But it has become so that complaints and requests for conversion have almost become the rule instead of the exception. It's easy to complain, and an incredible number do. Then one can ask the question about their complaints because they really think they are unfairly treated, because they do not know Norwegian law and legislation, or because we have lawyers who also need a living. I'm not going to give a clear answer, but I think it might be a combination of these relationships that are the right answer. It is, as has been said before, a challenge for the entire administration that there are so many opportunities to complain and send requests for conversion. This spends an incredible amount of time and resources on, time and money that could have been spent in a much better way, on faster and better processing of ordered applications, and not least on integrating those who have been granted their applications in a good way. In fact, many people stay in the country for a very long time after they have been rejected. Some come from the fact that there are difficult return conditions, that there is little willingness to return and that there are good complaints – or a combination of these. I am pleased that the government and the committee see that there are challenges in the handling of complaints and that we have a relatively long time to deal with them. I'm glad that the committee and the government want to do something about this. I look forward to the government making a communication in which it makes good, concrete proposals to limit the possibility of resumption, and that we will then have a reduced treatment period, so that we can use the resources in a good and sensible way, among other things. for integration. I refer to the proposals made by the Progress Party in the matter.

ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e3413	There seems to be a broad consensus that there is a problem with requests for conversion, which are not limited in number. ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e3422	The parties agree with the proposers that the high number of conversion requests is a serious challenge. While it is important to have a genuine means of handling complaints and to maintain the principle that cases should be dealt with in two instances in order to ensure legal certainty for individuals, it is also important to ensure sound and prudent procedures. On the other hand, when all negative decisions are requested, there is reason to take a critical review. It may seem to be a very little sensible resource use to allow all individual cases to be dealt with an unlimited number of times. Many believe that providing a change request means that they may stay in Norway until the case has been dealt with again. Fortunately, it's not like that. The exit obligation in case of refusal is maintained unless the case contains significant new information. Nor does it prevent a forced return from being carried out. The problem with too many requests for conversion was part of the mandate of the Complaints Board, which was set up in 2009, and the Department is now working on follow-up to the vacancy. In my opinion, it should be considered whether, within the international agreements, it is possible to lay down the conditions that a certain period must elapse between final rejection and the possibility of making a request for conversion, for example. one year. The possibility that there will be new elements in the matter will be greater the longer the period goes on. This could be done, for example, by: have changed conditions in the home Member State. It should also be discussed whether it is possible to set a ceiling on the number. Some have delivered a change request a whole ten times. This is as far as I know the only kind of thing that never sets a final point. At the same time, it is a fact that, in many cases, management has an obligation under international conventions to assess new evidence or other new pre-dispatch information. Norwegian rules, such as: The provision of the foreign regulation on the special importance of the link between children may give an obligation to assess conversion requests. The government has a clear objective of speeding up proceedings and faster return of persons with final rejection. Faster returns will hopefully reduce the number of conversion requests, as the chances of a change in conditions will be very small if the applicant is returned immediately after the rejection. The Central Party is pleased that the Council of State in its letter of reply is concerned with finding measures that may limit resource use and prevent requests for conversion from delaying the work on returns. I welcome the Council of State's proposal on how to follow up the Complaint Board's decision and hope that it will do so as soon as possible.
ParlaMint-NO_2012-11-20.ana.ud2487e3437	I agree that it is an important task to reduce resource use when processing conversion requests. Before I go into this, I would nevertheless like to stress that the most important measure to reduce the number of conversion requests, as several have mentioned, is to get quick returns from people who have been finally rejected. The return work is a priority area for the government. I have noticed that the proposers have argued that conversion requests ‘must not be allowed.’ This is in my opinion to go too far. It would be unacceptable to send someone back to persecution or assault. Government policy is to provide protection to those in need of protection. In addition, Norway is obliged under the Refugee Convention and the European Convention on Human Rights not to return to persecution or abuse if it is entitled to protection under those Conventions. In addition, the Convention on the Rights of the Child requires that we consider the best interests of the child in matters relating to children. If the foreign authorities do not assess any new evidence or other information that comes in before the return takes place, we risk sending someone back to prosecution or assault and thereby also breaking the Convention obligations. The first years after the UN was established, there was a rule that the principal could decide to reject information, documents or other evidence which could have been provided or submitted during the previous proceedings. In 2005, this provision was repealed on a proposal from the Bondevik II Government. This had the background to UNE’s finding it more appropriate to deal with groundless conversion requests with a brief reply that there was no basis for conversion. This provision had thus proved to be of no practical importance. Instead, on a proposal from the Bondevik II Government, a provision was introduced that conversion requests should only be accepted if the foreigner needed protection, or if there were ‘particularly strong human considerations’. This provision did not lead to any reduction in the number of conversion requests, nor did it have any particular effect on the number of conversions. The condition that there was a need for ‘particularly strong human considerations’ for conversion was lifted in 2007. The Board of Appeal has now proposed that UNE should not be able to make its own decisions, but that UDI should decide whether the case should be reopened. A condition for reopening shall be that new information or evidence of facts which have not previously been assessed has been submitted and that there must be more than 50 pst. likely that the new moments will lead to conversion. If the case is first reopened, but the UDI refuses to reopen it, the case should be brought before UNE again under the terms of the proposal. During the consultation round, UNE and UDI have indicated that the selection proposals could lead to an increase rather than a reduction in resource use. Furthermore, the UDI is critical of the sample’s assumption that the scheme will lead to fewer conversion requests and UNE believes that the scheme could lead to a higher number of legal proceedings. UNE also has a number of other objections to the proposal. When assessing the selection proposals in this way, both the relevant professional bodies also require a critical assessment by the department. It is also necessary to consider alternative schemes. One of the questions currently being examined by the Ministry is whether a separate authorisation can be given to reject requests for conversion where UNE considers that there is no significant new information. The proposers wish to investigate the establishment of an administrative court. At this point, I would like to refer to the experience gained from Sweden, where the responsibility for handling complaints has been transferred to the courts. In Sweden too, the challenges of conversion requests are not being met. Last year, the Finnish Immigration Service handled 11,653 enquiries on issues that were already settled. The Migration Agency has estimated that in 2011 they spent 45 years in the handling of such transformations. As I informed you instead, my department is now working on the follow-up to the Complaints Committee's investigation. I can inform both the representative of Greece and others that during the first half of 2013 I will therefore return to Stortinget, among others. concerning the processing of conversion requests.
